請參閱末頁之聲明

2026.11.11 
王章妮 C00251@ibfs.com.tw 
1476.TT 儒鴻 
 評價低緣、高殖利率，投資價值浮現

[法說會更新]   
   公司簡介 
儒鴻成立於 1977 年，生產各式針織布及成衣，從染整、織布、定型、成衣，擁有垂
直整合生產供應鏈，並切入機能性服飾市場，2025 年針織布營收 127.7 億元，YoY-
2.3%，營收占比 33.61%，成衣營收 252.2 億元，YoY+6.2%，比重占 66.39%。 
投資評等/目標價 重點摘要 
買進 416 
【 前 次 投 資 建 議 】買進，396 
【大盤指數/股價】42712 / 348.5

➢ 3Q26 獲利略低於投研部預估 
公司 3Q26 營收 105.72 億元，YoY+9.7%，營收略低於投研部預估之
106.8 億元，主要係因成衣營收低於投研部預期所致，毛利率 31.18%，
優於投研部預期，主要受惠高毛利率新產品及新客戶占比提升，且匯率
亦有所助益(約影響毛利率 0.62 個百分點)，費用率因業績獎金提列較高
而推升至 10.49%，營益率 20.69%，低於投研部預估之 21.24%，稅後
EPS 6.36 元，低於投研部預估之 6.57 元。 
➢ 4Q26 展望正向，略為上修 4Q26 獲利預估 
10/2026 營收 34 億元，YoY+10.7%，符合投研部預期，本次法說會管
理層表示對 4Q26 營運維持 TITAS 2026 釋出之看法不變 ，4Q26 營收可
望為年內高峰，上修 4Q26 營收預估至 105.99 億元，YoY+14.4%，毛
利率因原料成本負面影響趨緩，公司高毛利率新產品及新客戶占比持續
提升 ， 毛利率同步上修至31.05%，YoY+0.86ppt.， 預估稅後淨利17.25
億元，YoY+9%，稅後 EPS 上修至 6.29 元。目前訂單能見度維持 6 個
月 ， 展望正向 ， 期中選舉民主黨勝選預期對關稅壁壘政策可望有所趨緩 ，
且美伊戰爭亦可望有所緩解，市場不確定因素減少亦有利於整體經濟消
費動能，印尼廠一期、二期目前維持穩定獲利且產能滿載，投研部上修
2026 年營收預估至 408.77 億元，YoY+7.6%，毛利率因新產品、新客
戶營收占比持續提升，略為上修至 31.09%，YoY+2.45ppt.，稅後 EPS
上修至 24.99 元。  
➢ 2027 年營運成長可期，評價處歷史區間低緣且殖利率逾 5%  
公司目前始承接 04/2027 訂單，觀察美國總體經濟動能強勁，消費者支
出可望維持韌性，惟考量公司現有廠區產能趨於滿載，目前僅以原有產
能小規模擴充為主，投研部以過去幾年公司中性成長均值預估 2027 年
營收 431.95 億元，YoY+5.7%，毛利率因 4Q27 印尼新廠投產而略為承
壓， 預估2027 年毛利率 30.62%，YoY-0.47ppt.， 營益率預估20.74%，
YoY+0.01ppt.， 稅後EPS 預估由 26.19 元略為上修至 26.31 元 ， 投研部
以現金股利配發率 74.63%預估，現金股利殖利率可望達 5.35%，且依
未來四季合計稅後 EPS 估算目前評價仍處過往歷史區間低緣位置，長期
投資價值浮現 ， 投研部維持評價PER 16X 並以未來四季稅後 EPS 估算目
標價，目標價由 396 元上修至 416 元(4Q26~3Q27F PE 16X) ，維持買
進投資建議。

個股報告

儒鴻為國內成衣廠龍頭 
儒鴻成立於 1977 年，生產各式針織布及成衣，從染整、織布、定型、成衣，擁有垂
直整合生產供應鏈，並切入機能性服飾市場，2025 年針織布營收 127.7 億元，YoY-
2.3%，營收占比 33.61%，成衣營收 252.2 億元，YoY+6.2%，比重占 66.39%；營收
區域別來看，2025 年美洲營收占比 50.87%，YoY+4.5%，亞洲營收占比 35.19%，
YoY+2.6%， 歐洲營收占比10.29%，YoY+10.2%， 中東營收占比2.49%，YoY-17.5%，
紐澳營收占比 0.85%。YoY+16.2%，非洲營收占比 0.31%。YoY-64.7%。 
3Q26 獲利略低於投研部預估  
公司 3Q26 營收 105.72 億元，QoQ+4.8%，YoY+9.7%，營收略低於投研部預估之
106.8 億元 ， 主要係因成衣營收低於投研部預期所致 ， 針織布營收則優於投研部預期 ，
毛利率 31.18%，QoQ+1.17ppt.，YoY+2.52ppt.，優於投研部預估之 30.76%，主要
受惠高毛利率新產品及新客戶占比提升，且匯率亦有所助益(約影響毛利率 0.62 個百
分點)，費用率因業績獎金提列較高而推升至 10.49%，高於投研部預估之 9.53%，營
益率 20.69%，QoQ+1.51ppt.，YoY+1.59ppt.，低於投研部預估之 21.24%，業外則
有匯兌收入認列 1,327 萬元，稅後淨利 17.44 億元，QoQ+12.3%，YoY+5.6%，稅
後 EPS 6.36 元，低於投研部預估之 6.57 元。

個股報告 
4Q26 展望正向，略為上修 4Q26 獲利預估 
10/2026 營收 34 億元，MoM-3.1%，YoY+10.7%，YTD+5.9%，占投研部原預估
4Q26 營收達成率 33.4%，符合投研部預期，公司過去 10 月單月營收占 4Q26 營收
占比區間介於 29.5%~34.2%，本次法說會管理層表示對 4Q26 營運維持台北紡織展
(TITAS 2026)釋出之看法不變，以訂單來看，若出貨船期順利，則 4Q26 營收可望為
年內高 峰， 基此 ，投研 部 上修 4Q26 營收 預估 至 105.99 億 元，QoQ+0.3% ，
YoY+14.4%， 毛利率因原料成本負面影響趨緩 ，公司高毛利率新產品及新客戶占比持
續提升，毛利率亦同步上修至 31.05%，QoQ-0.14ppt.，YoY+0.86ppt.，並同步上修
營益率至 20.33%，QoQ-0.36ppt.，YoY+1.17ppt.， 預估稅後淨利17.25 億元 ，QoQ-
1.1%，YoY+9%，稅後 EPS 由 5.84 元略為上修至 6.29 元。 
展望 2026 年，訂單能見度維持 2 個季度，訂單展望維持正向，公司表示目前訂單尚
未因美國期中選舉結果出爐後而有所變動，然期中選舉民主黨勝選預期對關稅壁壘政
策可望有所趨緩，且美伊戰爭亦可望有所緩解，市場不確定因素減少亦有利於整體經
濟消費動能，印尼廠一期、二期目前維持穩定獲利且產能滿載，目前協力廠外發比重
維持 27%~28%，因 4Q26 營收及獲利略為上修，投研部亦同步上修 2026 年營收預
估至 408.77 億元，YoY+7.6%，毛利率部份，因高毛利率新產品、新客戶營收占比可
望 持 續 提 升 ， 且 匯 率 環 境 相 較 於2025 年 亦 較 為 友 善 ， 略 為 上 修31.09% ，
YoY+2.45ppt.，營益率則由 20.62%上修至 20.73%，YoY+2.06ppt.，並預估稅後淨
利 68.56 億元，YoY+24.3%，稅後 EPS 由 24.77 元上修至 24.99 元。 
圖 1：公司歷年月營收趨勢               單位：千元

資料來源：國票投顧彙製 
2027 年營運可望維持成長，目前評價處歷史區間低緣且殖利率逾 5% 
展望 2027 年，目前已開始承接 04/2027 訂單，雖訂單能見度未達 2027 年底，且客
戶端 2027 年預示單仍洽談中，然觀察美國就業環境仍穩健，初領及續領失業救濟金
人數仍處歷史低檔，總體經濟動能強勁，消費者支出可望維持韌性，且 2026 年營運
受戰爭、通膨影響較為劇烈，研判 2027 年可望較為淡化，惟考量公司現有廠區產能
趨於滿載，印尼三期實際貢獻營收時點落於 4Q27~1Q28，目前僅以原有產能小規模
擴充產能為主，若市況優於預期則以外發協力廠因應， 2027 年外發比重大致上介於
27%~35%區間 ， 基此 ， 投研部以過去幾年公司中性成長均值預估2027 年營收 431.95
億元，YoY+5.7%，主要為產品報價提升之貢獻，並預估毛利率因 4Q27 印尼新廠投

日本：7月以降の設備投資需要はAI関連産業を中心に旺盛
3
 7～9月期の機械受注は増勢回復へ。AI関連業種からの受注がけん引
― ①旺盛なAI需要のもと、関連業種では設備不足感が強まっていたこと、②輸出増が持続するもとで、好業績が期待され
ること、③中東情勢の不確実性が後退したことなどが背景
 先行き設備投資は緩やかな増勢を辿る見込み
― 半導体製造装置をはじめ、AI・データセンター関連品目を中心に受注残が増加しており、目先こうした傾向が続く見通し
― 積み上がった受注残を段階的に消化することで、設備投資が持続的に下支えされる公算が大きい

世界：AI関連投資の拡大が鉱物需要を押し上げる一方、鈍い資源開発投資の回復ペース
4
 AI関連需要の拡大が銅やレアアースの需給環境に影響
― データセンター建設や電力インフラ整備に伴い、銅やレアアースの需要が拡大。ガリウム等の戦略鉱物への需要も増加
 資源開発投資の回復が遅れるなか、中長期的な供給不足への懸念強まる
―IEAは、2035年に銅が供給量対比で約26％の供給不足に陥ると予測。レアアースは供給不足を回避する見込みながら、

採掘・精錬において中国が支配的地位を占めており、供給元の集中がリスク

世界：国債市場に残る脆弱性。インフレ・財政懸念による金利急騰リスクは増大
 主要国の長期金利は、期待短期金利とタームプレミアムの双方から上昇
―IMFは財政懸念などを背景にタームプレミアムが歴史的に高い領域にあると指摘
― 国債発行の増加や借換リスクの高まりに加え、価格感応度の高い投資家の存在がインフレ・財政ショックによる金利上
昇を増幅する可能性
― 国債市場は足元で秩序を維持するも、急激な金利上昇は資金調達市場の逼迫や銀行部門への波及を招くリスク

ドの存在感が増大。インフレが長

世界：ヘッジファンドのレバレッジ拡大が市場ショックを増幅、銀行部門への波及にも警戒
 高まるヘッジファンドの存在感と市場への影響力
― ヘッジファンドの総資産は2013年の約4兆ドルから2026年初には約13兆ドルに拡大。平時には市場の流動性や効率性
を支える一方、ストレス時にはレバレッジ解消や取引集中が価格変動を増幅させる懸念
 ヘッジファンドの損失がプライムブローカーを通じて銀行部門に波及するリスク
―IMFはヘッジファンドの運用成績悪化がプライムブローカーの収益にも悪影響を及ぼすと分析。銀行自身の健全性に加
え、ノンバンクとの相互連関を通じた金融システム全体への波及にも警鐘

欧州：EU・中国はハイブリッド車の輸入制限で暫定合意。同様の措置は他の品目に広がる可能性
11
 EUと中国は、貿易不均衡の是正措置について暫定合意。中国からのハイブリッド車輸入は従来見通しの半分以下に
―EUが中国産EVに追加関税を賦課した2024年以降、追加関税の対象とならないハイブリッド車輸入が急増
― 欧州委員会の試算では、今回の措置によって2030年までの4年間で数百万台の輸入を削減見込み。直近では、EUが輸
入するハイブリッド車の約半分を中国車が占めており、域内自動車市場に一定のインパクト

― 欧州委員会のモニタリング対象（輸入増加と価格下落が併存する品目）は、中国からの輸入品が最多

 フィリピンでAZEC（閣僚会合）とAEBFが同時期に開催。ASEANのエネルギー安全保障に関心が高まる

 ASEAN5では石油の国家備蓄制度や設備が未整備。石油の安定供給に向けた対応の強化が急務
―ASEANの場合、石油備蓄量は1～2カ月分と限定的。緊急時を見据えた石油備蓄の整備は途上
―10月のAZEC閣僚会合においては、ASEANの石油備蓄拡大や、緊急時の共同備蓄放出に向けた計画などで合意
 電力については域内での需要拡大が見込まれる中、発電能力の増強や送配電網の整備による電力供給の拡大が課題
―IEAは東南アジアの電力需要が2030年まで年平均5.4％増加すると予測。産業や冷房、データセンター需要が押し上げ
― 再エネ含む多様な電源による供給拡大や、AEBFでも議論された国際送配電網の整備や電力融通の取り組みが主眼に

 ASEANの消費は中東情勢緊迫化後に減速も、概して底堅く推移
― インドネシア・タイは4～6月期に大きめに落ち込んだものの足元で持ち直しの動き。フィリピン、マレーシアは減速が続く
も大崩れを回避、ベトナムは堅調さを維持
 背景に各国個別要因や消費マインド悪化の歯止め
― タイは消費刺激策（6～11月）、マレーシアはインフレの抑制、ベトナムは高い賃金上昇率が消費を下支え
― 消費マインドは中東情勢緊迫化後の悪化に歯止め。もっとも、中東危機の長期化が重しに
アジア：ASEANの消費は足元で総じて底堅い動き

日本：コメ価格下落で企業物価は鈍化。もっとも、先行きは原油高を背景に再加速へ
18
 9月の国内企業物価はコメ価格の下落を主因に、前年比＋6.8％（前月：同＋7.6％）と鈍化
― 新米価格が昨年を下回ったこと等を背景に、コメ価格が大幅に下落。国内企業物価を▲0.75%Pt押し下げ
― 一方、3月来の原油高を背景に化学・プラスチック製品は高止まり。また、AI需要等を背景に非鉄金属も高い伸びを維持
 7月以降の原油高再燃を背景に、先行きの国内企業物価は再加速する見込み
― 原油価格高止まりにタンカー運賃の急騰が重なり、先行きの輸入物価は原油関連を中心に加速へ
― 国内企業物価は石油・石炭製品やラグを伴いつつ化学・プラスチック製品などを中心に再加速する見込み

Accton Technology Corporation  | Asia Pacific
October Sales +11% MoM/+91%
YoY
In this report, we focus on Accton's monthly sales, which we
believe could be a catalyst for share price movement.
Details:
• October sales were NT$44,663mn (+11% MoM/+91% YoY).
• QTD reached 35% of our 4Q26 estimate at NT$126,937mn (+5% QoQ/+76%
YoY) and 36% of consensus at NT$122,585mn (+2% QoQ/+70% YoY).
Our view:
• We see its 4Q26 revenue tracking slightly ahead of market expectations.

• We expect Accton to continue growing its revenue and gross margin
sequentially in 4Q26 amid solid demand from customers, although
component supply could be a potential gating factor.
• Stay OW.

Morgan Stanley Research 3
Valuation Methodology and Risks
Accton Technology Corporation (2345.TW)
Base case, P/E, which we believe better captures the company's earnings growth momentum.
We assign a target P/E of 30x to our 2027 EPS estimate, which we view as justified by an
operating profit CAGR of 48% for 2025-28, based on our estimates. This implies a PEG of
around 0.6, vs. its average of 0.7 over 2019-25, when the stock was trading at a P/E of 22x for

an operating profit CAGR of 32%.
Risks to Upside
n Stronger data center capex
n Faster network switch spec migration
n InfiniBand losing share in AI networks
n Stronger custom AI accelerator growth
n Stronger ODM direct business model adoption
Risks to Downside
n Weaker data center capex
n Slower network switch spec migration
n InfiniBand taking more share in AI networks
n Softer custom AI accelerator growth
n Softer ODM direct business model adoption

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

## Document 160: 261110_gs_kinsus

Source PDF: all report pdf/261110_gs_kinsus.pdf

### Page 1

Key Data  _____________________________________

GS Forecast  __________________________________

GS Factor Profile  ______________________________

We hosted Kinsus’ 3Q26 post-result follow-up call today (Nov 
10th). Key takeaways include: 
(1) Kinsus mentioned it is getting more market share from existing 
server CPU customers, AI GPU customer, and ASIC customers. 
Despite not having much market share gain opportunity in its AI 
server CPU products, the company mentioned the volume growth 
could still be 150% YoY in AI server CPU shipment in 2027 vs. 2026, 
suggesting a good revenue/GM growth outlook going into 2027/28.  
(2) Kinsus guides 2027/28 revenue to go up by at least 30% YoY 
each year while GM should be at least 35/40% in 2027/28, 
considering the orders/capacity/UTR/product mix differences, 
despite the key upside risk will be on like for like base pricing hike 
(the guidance above does not include any pricing hike assumption, 
but this could lift the revenue growth by another 15ppt in both 
years although not guaranteed, per the company). 
(3) For 4Q26, the company guided revenue to go up by 10% QoQ, 
with GM being expanded into 35% (from ~34% in 3Q26), despite a 
much more favorable than 3Q product mix and ~5-10% pricing hike 
on ABF & BT substrates. For 1Q27, the company expects some 
seasonal issues on consumer electronics and contact lens, and 
believes revenue should be flat to slightly up QoQ, despite possible 
GM slowdown during the low season. 
(4) In terms of the future investment plan, Kinsus mentioned it will 
only need to invest in the land/infrastructure/construction etc. while 
end customers will take care of the equipment; normally, equipment 
accounted for 60% of the total CAPEX while the rest only accounts 
for 40% of the total investment.  
(5) Kinsus mentioned it now holds ~70% of the AI server CPU 
substrate market share, and the company believes its market share 
should be pretty stable in this product cycle. For AI GPU, Kinsus

GM to further expand in 2027/28 with a better high-end ABF exposure/market share,

mentioned it expects its share to increase from 10% now to 30% in 2027 for its existing
customer’s supply chain. Kinsus also mentioned it is working on several ASIC customers’
substrates product, which could also be the key revenue/profit driver for 2027 and
beyond.
(6) In terms of the order forecast, the company mentioned it has not yet seen any order
cut or order delay on its ABF substrates business, but the company continues to
maintain its cautious but positive view on the overall substrate industry outlook.

Investment view
We are positive on Kinsus’ 3Q result (GM expanded by 7.8ppt QoQ in 3Q - see here), and
believe the company’s GM expansion trend will continue in coming quarters, despite the
company only guiding 1ppt QoQ growth on GM in 4Q (however, the company is also
giving a conservative guidance on profitability. Kinsus only guided GM to be 27-28% in
3Q26, but it ended up with 33.9% in its 3Q result). Moreover, we believe Kinsus’
proactive capacity expansion plan (1 new plant every year from 2025 to 2029) will start

to help the company to outperform peers from 2Q27 (the new K6-B plant starts
contributing revenue from 2Q27) which should also help the company to enjoy better
profitability in coming quarters, as the new plants will focus on high-end projects, whose
GM is generally 10ppt+ higher than the company’s average level).
Overall, we maintain our Buy rating on Kinsus, and we expect the better market share in
AI server CPU and its better position in the high-end ABF substrate industry to

strengthen into the long term.
We maintain our Buy rating with an unchanged 12m TP of NT$1,515, based on the
same 19.5x 2028E EPS, which is +2.0x STDV higher than Kinsus’ past upcycle average
P/E (14.2x).
Investment Thesis - Kinsus
Kinsus is one of the key IC substrates suppliers globally with ~10% ABF/BT substrates
market share in 2024, with ~18% of total 2025 revenue contributed by its contact lens
subsidiary, Pegavision (6941.TW, Not Covered). With our view that the ABF substrate

shortage started from 2Q26 (ABF substrate return to supply demand balance from late
2025), we believe Kinsus’ performance will continue to improve compared with high-end
ABF peers considering the company’s increasing market share in the high-end ABF
substrate market; however, ~36% of the company’s revenue was contributed by BT
substrates in 2025 and 30% of the company’s ABF revenue is from non-LTA customers,
which we believe will continue to enjoy a better pricing outlook. As a result, we believe

Kinsus’ earnings outlook should continue to be strong (~118% 2026-29E CAGR), mainly
driven by a better BT substrate and non-LTA ABF substrate pricing outlook, despite the
unfavorable demand outlook before 1H26. We believe Kinsus is an experienced
substrate supplier with a clear capacity expansion plan that can also upgrade its product
portfolio in the long term. We believe we are at the beginning of an industry upcycle,
making us positive on Kinsus’ stock performance over the next 12 months.

Price Target Risks and Methodology - Kinsus
Valuation methodology: Our 12-month target price of NT$1,515 for Kinsus is based on
19.5x 2028E P/E, which is +2.0x STDV higher than Kinsus’ past upcycle average P/E

Ratings, coverage universe and related definitions
Buy (B), Neutral (N), Sell (S) Analysts recommend stocks as Buys or Sells for inclusion on various regional Investment Lists. Being assigned a Buy or Sell
on an Investment List is determined by a stock’s total return potential relative to its coverage universe. Any stock not assigned as a Buy or a Sell on an
Investment List with an active rating (i.e., a  stock that is not Rating Suspended, Not Rated, Early-Stage Biotech, Coverage Suspended or Not Covered), is

deemed Neutral. Each region manages Regional Conviction Lists, which are selected from Buy rated stocks on the respective region’s Investment Lists
and represent investment recommendations focused on the size of the total return potential and/or the likelihood of the realization of the return across
their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

Total return potential represents the upside or downside differential between the current share price and the price target, including all paid or
anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.

 절대 (달러환산) (5.2) (2.7) 0.2

3Q26 영업이익 1.8조, 핵심 하락 원인은 인센티브 
환율효과는 전분기 대비 -5,330억 작용했으나, 기말환율 하락에 따른 판매보증비의 급감 
(6,200억, -9,250억 qoq)으로 상쇄되었다 판단. 인센티브 비용 증가세는 -6,060억원의 영
향을 미쳤으며, 대부분 유럽지역 EV2 판매를 위한 딜러 인센티브에서 발생 추정. 
외생변수는 능력 밖의 일, EV 전략에 대해서는 장기성장에 무게 
중동 분쟁 지속, 환율 하락과 같은 외생변수의 부정적 영향을 제외한다면 단기 영업적 측면
의 실적 악재는 BEV 인센티브 지급기조의 변화가 없을 것이라는 점. 중장기 글로벌 BEV 
시장의 입지 강화를 단기 전사 수익성보다 중시하는 전략을 채택. 
마찬가지로, 4분기가 환율 효과의 본격화 
3Q26과 반대로 qoq 기말환율 대비 낙폭 확대할 평균환율 (QTD 1,338원) 효과로 환관련 
감익 본격화 예상. 즉, 판매보증비의 정상화 (qoq 반등)로 인해 수익성 추가 악화 가능성 존
재. 완성차 판매량의 경우 경쟁사 대비 견조했던 2Q-3Q26의 기조를 유지할 것이라 예상. 
투자의견 BUY 유지, 목표주가 129,000원으로 하향 
목표주가 하향 근거는 산정 기준을 FY27F로 변경함과 동시에 FY27F 자동차사업 NOPAT 
예상치 하향과 현대모비스 지분가치 하락. 밸류에이션상 저점에 도달했다는 의견은 FY27F 
EPS의 yoy 하락으로 설득력 약화. 중장기 BEV 시장 입지 강화에 대한 밸류에이션 프리미
엄 반영은 글로벌 전동화 속도에 달려 있음. 주가 상승 트리거는 1) 외생변수의 개선, 2) 신
차 출시를 통한 상품성 강화, 3) BEV 시장 점유율 확대 이후 점진적 인센티브 축소, 4) 20조
원 수준의 순현금 기반 발생 가능한 사업적 결정.

The Japan Research Institute, Limited  
Economics Department

1 
Topics  Regulatory reform in India's insurance sector

Over the past quarter century, the Indian government has pursued the liberalization of the country's insurance market, 
with the aim of bringing stability to people's living standards and strengthening financial intermediation functions. 
Recently, however, it has begun to reverse course, tightening rules in order to better protect consumers.

■ India's insurance market has grown rapidly with deregulation 
The Indian government has long pursued 
the development of the country's insurance 
market, with the aim of bringing stability to 
people's living standards and strengthening 
financial intermediation functions. This 
stance can be attributed to two background 
factors:  
The first is that India's social security 
system remains underdeveloped. As a result, 
enrollment in health and life insurance can 
provide a vital safety net for mitigating the 
risk of sharp declines in household income 
due to unexpected illnesses or accidents.  
The second is that the sustainable 
development of the Indian economy requires 
the accumulation of long -term and stable 
domestic capital, separate from short -term 
bank deposits, in order to  finance the 
development of infrastructure and the 
nurturing of industry. The hope has been that 
the enormous sums of money collected and 
managed by insurance companies can serve 
as a reliable source for this long -term 
financing.  
These factors have led the Indian 
government to walk a path of gradual 
deregulation over the past quarter century. 
The process began with the liberalization of 
market entry by private and foreign 
companies in 2000, which was followed by 
a lifting of the ba n on "bancassurance" (the 
sale of insurance through bank branches) in 
2002, the raising of the ceiling on foreign 
ownership, and other reforms.  
This series of deregulatory measures 
spurred the entry of numerous private and foreign 
firms, causing market competition to intensify. Several 
Japanese insurers, including Nippon Life and the Tokio 
Marine Group, also entered the market, helping to drive 
investment in India.  
Besides deregulation related to market entry and 
sales, other factors also contributed to the rapid growth 
of India's insurance market, namely the expansion of 
the middle class, improvements in household financial 
literacy, and the digitalization of insura nce services. 
Premium income grew at a pace exceeding economic 
growth, and in recent years insurance companies' assets 
under management have reached a level equivalent to

incentives to sell products and inappropriate sales practices. This should shore up the credibility of the 
insurance market and the foundation for its sustainable development. 
On the minus side, there are worries about side effects arising from the transition. As rules on selling 
become stricter, insurance companies and the financial institutions acting as insurance agents will inevitably 
see some temporary moderation in  the pace of their earnings growth. Moreover, given that insurance 
companies and financial institutions are major investors in India's financial markets, tighter regulation is 
likely to exert downward pressure on equity and bond prices. 
These changes in the insurance market provide an important perspective for assessing the future 
trajectory of the Indian economy. They illustrate that rapid market expansion driven by liberalization can 
create a variety of problems. And this dynamic is not  limited to the insurance sector, as it can also occur 
across many other industries. At the same time, the redesign and recalibration of regulations in response to 
such developments can provide a foundation for a new stage of growth. The new insurance regu lations 
could constitute an early example of this phenomenon, so will be closely watched to see what changes they 
bring in the years ahead.

Topics  Talent travel curbs may weigh on China’s growth

In September, the Chinese government introduced new exit-entry rules. Indicative of an effort to retain talent in order 
to prevent technology leakage, the rules could narrow opportunities for China to absorb overseas knowledge and weigh 
on its economic growth over the medium to long term.

■ Scope of overseas travel restrictions expanded 
On September 15, the Chinese 
government brought in new rules 
specifying conditions and screening 
procedures for entry and exit. The new 
rules explicitly state that the Ministry of 
Commerce or other departments may 
prohibit Chinese citizens from leaving the  
country if they violate export -control or 
technology import/export regulations and 
are deemed likely to threaten national 
industrial or technological security.  
Under China's Exit and Entry 
Administration Law, which came into 
effect in 2013, defendants and suspects in 
criminal cases, as well as persons involved 
in unsettled civil cases whom courts have 
prohibited from leaving the country, had 
already been subject to exit restrictions. In 
addition, Chinese citizens could be denied 
permission to leave the country if the 
competent departments determined that 
their departure might endanger national 
security or interests. Aside from formal exit 
bans, personnel who handl e state secrets 
and officials in the public sector have long 
been subject to controls such as centralized 
passport management and the need to 
obtain approval in advance of overseas travel. What the new rules effectively do is make it clear that exit 
can also be restricted when there are issues from the standpoint of economic security. 
Against this backdrop, it was reported at the end of September that overseas travel restrictions already 
imposed on key personnel in the AI and semiconductor sectors had been expanded to include their spouses, 
children, and other family members. According to these reports, these key personnel include prominent AI 
startup founders, senior executives at major private enterprises, and core R&D talent. Although the measure 
does not constitute a blanket travel ban, affected individuals and their families are rep ortedly required to 
obtain government approval for exit even in the case of short private trips or business travel.  
This regulatory tightening is thought to be related to the technological rivalry between the U.S. and China. 
As competition surrounding critical technologies such as generative AI intensifies, the Chinese government 
appears to view not only technologies themselves, but also the researchers and engineers who create them, 
as strategic assets. By widening travel curbs to encompass family members, the authorities are likely 
seeking to prevent overseas emigration by individuals or the relocation of R&D operation s abroad. A 
noteworthy aspect of the recent reports is that the controls apparently now extend beyond the public sector 
to key personnel at private enterprises, as well as their families. Restrictions like these on talent mobility 
could undermine the mecha nisms of technology absorption and productivity improvement that have 
hitherto facilitated China's growth.

■ China's productivity gains have been supported by market opening and technology 
absorption 
Productivity exerts a substantial influence on 
economic growth. Total factor productivity (TFP), an 
indicator of an economy's overall production 
efficiency, provides an estimate of efficiency in 
production that cannot be explained solely in terms of 
inputs of capital and labor, and reflects factors such as 
levels of technology and efficiency of resource 
allocation. 
China's TFP has been rising for many years. In 
addition to the transition to a market economy and the 
growth of private enterprises since the country adopted 
the policy of reform and opening up , Chinese 
productivity has benefitted from the absorption of 
foreign technology and managerial know‑how through 
trade and direct investment. A labor shift from 
agriculture to manufacturing and other sectors, along 
with competition among firms and the weeding out of 
weaker players, has also helped deliver productivity 
gains across the economy.  
Nevertheless, China's TFP remains low relative to 
other major economies. According to estimates from 
Penn World Table, China's TFP in 2023 was less than 
half that of the U.S., and it was also below the levels not 
only of Germany and other major European ec onomies 
and of South Korea and Japan, but of Thailand and 
Malaysia, too. Factors behind this include inefficient 
resource allocation and the slow diffusion of advanced 
technologies and management practices. Preferential 
financial and institutional treatmen t for state‑owned 
enterprises, regional protectionism, and mechanisms 
that impede market entry and exit have distorted the 
allocation of resources, preventing capital and labor 
from being fully utilized by more productive firms and sectors. Moreover, although China has achieved 
strong competitiveness in certain advanced industries, such as electric vehicles and semiconductors, the 
leading-edge technologies and management practice s developed in these sectors have not sufficiently 
diffused to the service sect or and small and medium‑sized enterprises, which form the foundation of the 
economy, and this has constrained overall productivity growth.  
■ Moves to contain technology could hinder growth over the medium to long term 
Efforts to retain talent in order to  prevent technology leakage could further delay China's productivity 
catch‑up with advanced economies. While restrictions on overseas travel by elite professionals may help 
with the retention of domestic R&D talent and protection of current technological a dvantages, they could 
also reduce opportunities to absorb overseas knowledge and to jointly develop new technologies with 
foreign partners. The transfer of technology and know‑how does not occur solely through the sharing of 
"explicit knowledge," in the fo rm of academic papers, technical blueprints, and so on. "Tacit knowledge," 
which is transmitted through face‑to‑face discussion, collaborative work, and the acquisition of experience 
in production settings, is also crucial. If travel controls delay or prev ent participation in international 
conferences, joint research projects, and technology exchanges with overseas companies, opportunities to 
absorb such knowledge may be lost, slowing technological innovation and improvements in production 
processes. Furthermore, if obtaining travel approval becomes more time‑consuming or less predictable, the 
overseas assignment or international deployment of technical personnel could also be obstructed.  
The new rules also cover the entry of foreign nationals. If uncertainty surrounding the administration of 
entry and exit controls increases, it could create obstacles to attracting and retaining highly skilled talent 
from abroad. At a time when inefficient resource allocation and the slow diffusion of advanced knowledge 
are already weighing on productivity growth in China, curbs on people-to- people exchange could further

impede both the absorption and creation of knowledge, as well as its practical application. Although the

restrictions on talent exchanges and overseas travel that reflect excessive emphasis on technology 
protection could slow China's TFP growth over the medium to long term and act as a headwind to the 
Chinese economy by lowering its potential growth rate.

IN THIS ISSUE
Macro Strategy—Guide to El Niño: What It Is, Why It Matters, and What to Do
About It: As crude oil flirts with April’s highs, and the price of diesel sits comfortably
above $6 per gallon, disruption to the global energy complex has emerged as a major
theme of 2026. Add yet another variable to the equation: the current “El Niño” event,

on track to be one of the strongest on record. The upshot: more extreme weather
from droughts to heavy rains, warmer temperatures globally, lower crop production in
commodity-exporting economies, and higher prices across major agricultural products.
Importantly, these pressures won’t be equally distributed and will largely depend on the
magnitude and timing of weather events. Among the most vulnerable will be heavily
import-dependent countries in the Middle East, Africa and parts of Asia, while developed

economies that count food as a smaller share of consumer spending should be better
insulated. For investors, the prospect of a very strong El Niño only further reinforces our
conviction in several structural trends including a return to higher inflation and higher
interest rates, elevated commodity prices amid heightened geopolitical risk and supply
chain shortages, and greater investment in energy infrastructure and supply chain
resilience. In portfolios, we favor commodities as both an inflation hedge and diversifier.

Market View—Another Tale of Market Rotation: October offered another reminder
that headline index returns do not always tell the full story. The S&P 500 ended the month
slightly lower, but the more muted headline return masked meaningful shifts in market
leadership beneath the surface. At the center of the volatility was a sharp unwind in the
Artificial Intelligence (AI) trade, particularly across semiconductors and memory stocks. Yet
pressure on AI market leaders has not translated into a broad retreat from U.S. Equities.

Instead, capital has moved toward previously lagging areas of the market, with Value-
oriented, defensive and rate-sensitive stocks gaining ground. While it remains too early to
determine whether the shift will prove durable, October’s rotation may reflect less a retreat
from the AI theme and more a recalibration of valuations and expected returns as market
leadership broadens. For investors, the speed of the shift reinforces the importance of
diversification as the bull market enters a potentially more mature and differentiated phase.

Thought of the Week—Reorienting Around AI: From Corporate Plans to Construction
to Capital Goods: AI is reshaping the physical economy, and companies are reorienting
capacity and investment plans to meet surging demand for data centers, power and
infrastructure. Industrial firms are adding or reviving capacity, while data center construction
has increased more than 60% over the past year and is outpacing other major construction
categories. At the same time, robust capital goods orders and long lead times for inputs such

as generators, switchboards, chillers and transformers highlight both the strength of demand
and the supply constraints the buildout faces. Taking stock of these signposts, our view
is reinforced that the AI investment cycle remains in its middle innings, creating potential
opportunities for companies supplying the equipment and infrastructure behind the buildout.

MACRO STRATEGY
Guide to El Niño: What It Is, Why It Matters, and What to Do About It
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
As crude oil flirts with April’s highs, and the price of diesel sits comfortably above $6 per
gallon, disruption to the global energy complex has emerged as a major theme of 2026.
Add yet another variable to the equation: the current “El Niño” event, on track to be one

of the strongest on record. Below we answer frequently asked questions on El Niño,
the potential impact on the global economy and markets and, most importantly, how
investors can position portfolios accordingly.
What is El Niño, and what makes this time different? El Niño is the warm phase
of the “El Niño-Southern Oscillation,” a natural climate pattern occurring every two to
seven years. Simply put, El Niño refers to the warming of sea surface temperatures in

the eastern Pacific, historically leading to drier conditions in some areas (Southeast Asia,
Australia, Central America, southern Africa) and wetter conditions in others (southern
U.S., parts of Latin America) (Exhibit 1).
What makes this episode different? Should forecasts prove correct, this El Niño is on
track to be historic. The National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration’s latest
outlook assigns at least an 83% probability that El Niño’s strength during October-

December will exceed that of any previous episode since 1950. Already, its impact has
been felt via wildfires in Indonesia, the weakest monsoon season in India since 2015, 1
and severe flooding from Paraguay to Peru. The upshot: more extreme weather, hotter
temperatures globally, lower crop production in commodity-exporting economies, and
higher prices across major agricultural products.
Exhibit 1: El Niño's Impact Varies Globally.
Geography Typical rainfall pattern Specific impacts

South Asia Weaker summer monsoon risk Lower crop yields, water shortages, food inflation
Southeast Asia Drier Lower rice and palm oil production, wildfire risk, food inflation
United States Wetter in the south; warmer/drier in the north Southern flood risk; lower northern heating demand
Central America & Northern South America Drier Drought, lower crop yields, water shortages, food inflation, lower hydropower output
Southern Africa Drier Weaker maize harvests, food inflation, lower hydropower output

Western South America Wetter along the Pacific coast Flooding, infrastructure damage, disrupted fisheries
Southern South America Wetter Flooding and crop damage; potential yield gains in some areas
Australia Drier Drought, agricultural losses, elevated wildfire risk, food inflation
Source: National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration, World Meteorological Organization, Food and Agriculture Organization, Australian Bureau of Meteorology, World Bank. Data as of

October 30, 2026.
How will El Niño impact the global economy and markets? Which regions will be
most affected? El Niño’s impact on the global economy and markets is likely to be felt
most via commodity price inflation. Alongside higher energy and fertilizer costs resulting
from war in Europe and the Middle East, El Niño could put further upward pressure on
commodities like wheat, sugar, corn, soy, cocoa and coffee as drought (or extreme rain)
weighs on crop yields and production. Latest estimates from the United Nations Food

and Agriculture Organization (FAO) suggest global food prices were up 5.8% year-over-
year (YoY) in September (Exhibit 2). Livestock is also likely to be challenged by higher
temperatures globally, while flooding has already threatened copper production in
Chile. Meanwhile, with hydropower accounting for more than half of the average annual
electricity generation in Brazil, Colombia, Peru and other Latin American countries, 2
below-average rainfall in affected areas has huge implications for electricity generation.

As an example, Colombia’s natural gas demand nearly doubled during the 2023-2024 El
Niño event.2 Another key watchpoint will be whether drier conditions reduce water levels
1 India Meteorological Department, Bloomberg. Refers to rainfall between June and September as of September 30, 2026.
2 International Energy Agency, as of October 7, 2026
Investment Implications
We expect El Niño to place upward
pressure on agricultural goods
prices globally. We continue to

see commodities as a strategic
diversifier and hedge against inflation
in portfolios. The U.S. remains our
preferred region with companies in
Industrials and Materials as potential
beneficiaries. Meanwhile, economic
impacts to the emerging world will vary
by country but the broader Emerging
Market (EM) equity market could be
less exposed given its significant tilt
toward Technology.

### Page 3

3 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
in the Panama Canal and other key shipping routes, particularly in light of the Strait of
Hormuz closure.
These pressures won’t be equally distributed and will heavily depend on this El Niño’s
severity. Among the more vulnerable to higher agricultural prices will be heavily import-
dependent countries in the Middle East, Africa, and parts of Asia. So too will economies
that count food as a larger share of consumer spending such as the Philippines (36%),

Indonesia (33%), Egypt (33%), India (28%), and Vietnam (26%) (Exhibit 3). Developed
economies like the U.S. (6%), Canada (10%), and Germany (11%) stand to be better
insulated from a food-related shock to headline inflation.
Within the U.S., some impacts could be positive with a potentially milder winter reducing
heating costs and wetter conditions in the South improving agricultural conditions. A recent
paper from the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco found that, in addition to putting

upward pressure on personal consumption expenditures (PCE) inflation, strong El Niño
episodes have been historically associated with a temporary boost to employment in the
following year via better construction conditions in the North and weather-event recovery
efforts.3 In California and parts of the Southwest, El Niño will be yet another stress test for
property insurers as flooding and landslide risks increase.
For more vulnerable developing economies, meanwhile, higher food and energy costs could

present a policy dilemma. On one hand, governments in vulnerable areas may move to
introduce fiscal relief to support households and boost growth. History points to plenty of
examples of this, such as Indonesia’s cash assistance in late 2023 or Thailand’s $1.8 billion
in loans to farmers in 2015. On the other hand, elevated inflation expectations and, in
some cases, weaker currencies could limit EM central banks’ room to ease monetary policy,
particularly with several major developed central banks aligned in hiking interest rates. Also

complicating matters: Global sovereign debt surpassed $110 trillion in Q2 2026, leaving
less fiscal space to absorb incoming shocks. 4
How can investors position portfolios accordingly? In many ways, the prospect of a
very strong El Niño only reinforces structural trends we’ve identified as defining the coming
years: a return to higher inflation and higher interest rates; elevated commodity prices
amid heightened geopolitical risk and resource/supply shortages; and greater investment in

energy infrastructure and supply chain resilience.
Considering these themes, we continue to like commodities not just as a hedge against
higher inflation but also as a diversification tool in portfolios given their negative correlation
with Equities and bonds. That’s not to mention secular forces placing upward pressure on
commodity prices such as the resource-intensive AI buildout and broader protectionism. To
wit, the Bloomberg Commodity Index, which spans oil, gold, various metals and agricultural

goods has gained nearly 30% this year, handily outperforming the S&P 500 (+1%) and the
Bloomberg U.S. Aggregate Bond Index (-1%).
Within Equities, the U.S. is still our preferred region. While upside risk to inflation remains
a risk to watch going into 2027, food is a relatively small share of the consumer basket in
the U.S., and neither strong services spending nor capital investment should be materially
affected by El Niño. Companies within the Industrials and Materials sectors focused on

precision agriculture, water management and crop protection may be positioned to benefit.
Import-dependent EM economies remain particularly exposed, though impacts will vary
widely depending on the magnitude and timing of weather events. It’s worth noting too that
EM Equities remain heavily concentrated in East Asian markets with substantial exposure to
technology, leaving the broader index arguably less exposed to the economic ramifications
of El Niño than in past episodes. Indeed, even after recent weakness in semiconductor

names, Taiwan, Korea, and China together account for a near-70% of the broader MSCI
Emerging Markets Index. 5 We maintain a slight overweight to the region in portfolios given
earnings strength but favor an active approach.

MARKET VIEW
Another Tale of Market Rotation
Kirsten Cabacungan, Vice President and Senior Investment Strategist
October offered another reminder that headline index returns do not always tell the
full story. The S&P 500 ended the month slightly lower, down almost 1%, but the more
muted headline return masked meaningful shifts in market leadership beneath the
surface. At the center of the volatility was a sharp unwind in the AI trade, particularly

across semiconductors and memory stocks, with the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index
experiencing a 19% intramonth drawdown. The dominant concerns in recent months
have centered on the sustainability of AI infrastructure spending and whether the scale
of investment will ultimately generate sufficient returns. Those only intensified amid a
sharp rise in bond yields after the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield spiked above 5.3% as of
October 5 for the first time since 2002, raising scrutiny over AI financing conditions and

whether higher borrowing costs could increase hurdle rates for continued investment
and slow the pace of capital spending.
Yet the pressure on AI market leaders has not translated into a broad retreat from U.S.
Equities. Instead, capital has moved toward previously lagging areas of the market,
with Value-oriented, defensive and rate-sensitive stocks gaining ground. Value defeated
Growth in October, with the Russell 1000 Value Index outperforming its Growth

counterpart by 10 percentage points, an outcome seen in only about 1% of months since
1979. The move extends Value's outperformance for the year, with the index up 23%
year-to-date compared with down almost 1% for Growth. Participation also improved.
The equal-weighted S&P 500 outperformed its market-cap-weighted counterpart by its
widest monthly gap since 2009 (Exhibit 4A), while the share of S&P 500 constituents
trading above their 200-day moving average rebounded to 62% from just 43% at the

end of September, and 76% of stocks outperformed the index for the month.

The latest rotation is not the first attempt at a change in market leadership this year.
The more important question is what that shift represents: a fundamental reassessment
of the AI trade or another step toward broader, more mature market leadership?
Recent Q3 Technology earnings results have sharpened that debate. Investors are
increasingly looking beyond the scale of AI spending to companies’ ability to translate
that investment into durable growth and attractive returns. The result may be greater

dispersion not only between Growth and Value, but also among AI leaders as investors
become more discerning about which companies can justify the scale of their
investments.
Investment Implications
We continue to look for potential
missing opportunities and favor
diversification across and within asset
classes in a multi-asset portfolio.
In particular, we see potential
opportunities to broaden exposure
beyond AI leaders toward areas
where earnings, capital spending and

productivity trends are improving,
including Value, Small- and Mid-caps,
and select non-U.S. markets, particularly
EMs and Japan. As market leadership
evolves, maintaining diversified
exposures and periodically rebalancing
portfolios can help investors participate
in a broader set of possible return
drivers without becoming overly
dependent on any single theme.

### Page 5

5 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
But greater selectivity does not necessarily signal the end of the AI investment cycle.
YoY growth rates may become harder to maintain after two years of rapid acceleration
as the comparison base rises, but absolute levels of AI capital spending remain elevated.
What matters more is whether spending translates into strong productivity and earnings
growth ahead and whether AI demand continues to support investment. October’s

volatility may therefore reflect less a retreat from the AI theme and more a recalibration
of valuations and expected returns, as other areas of the market begin to contribute
more meaningfully to market performance.
Several developments ahead will help determine whether that rotation has staying
power. One important test is whether broader participation can persist if pressure on AI
stocks continues. Continued strength outside the AI trade would support the case that

investors are reallocating capital rather than broadly reducing risk. The path of monetary
policy will also matter as investors assess where borrowing costs may be headed. The
Federal Reserve’s not-so-hawkish hold in October offered some reassurance, but the
path ahead remains dependent on the evolving balance between inflation and growth.
Any signs of resolution of geopolitical tensions remain an important swing factor
particularly as recent flashpoints in the Middle East have pressured energy prices higher

again. Results of the midterm elections should also provide greater visibility into the
policy landscape for the next few years. While the market does not need every clearing
event to resolve favorably, greater clarity around these uncertainties could help narrow
the range of potential outcomes facing investors.
For investors, October’s sudden change in leadership reinforces the importance of
diversification. Leadership can shift quickly, leaving portfolios that become overly

dependent on a single theme, sector or style particularly vulnerable when it does. We
remain constructive on U.S. Equities and believe that the market may be transitioning
more toward a mid-cycle phase in which returns become broader but also more
measured compared to the sharp acceleration earlier in the cycle. That argues not for
abandoning the market’s long-term winners, but for complementing them with exposure
to additional sources of returns across style, size, sector and region.

### Page 6

6 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
Reorienting around AI: From Corporate Plans to Construction to Capital Goods
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
For all the questions (and recent market weakness) hanging over the AI trade, from
monetization and cash burn to financing risk and overcapacity, a measurable aspect is
the buildout itself. Data center construction is lifting construction spending and capital

goods orders, while companies are adding and reviving capacity to meet AI-related
demand. Still, planning delays, access to power, labor scarcity, local politics and the cost
of capital remain meaningful constraints on the buildout’s pace or profitability.
Corporate Plans. Take Caterpillar as one example that has resumed production of a
10-megawatt natural gas turbine discontinued back in 2022. A 175-year old glassmaker,
Corning, is leaning into optical connectivity as AI clusters require faster connections of

optical fiber cabling between servers. Caterpillar and Corning are just two companies
among others pivoting to supply data centers.
Construction to Capital Goods. The AI reorientation is evident in construction
spending as well, where data center construction has outpaced every other major
residential and nonresidential category. Over the last year AI-related construction on data
centers has surged more than 60% while spending on residential, lodging, commercial

or office has declined notably (Exhibit 5A). Data center construction is running at an $85
billion (B) annualized rate this year, versus roughly $49B a year earlier, and now accounts
for 11% of private nonresidential construction, up from just 2% in 2022 according to
the Census Bureau. Similarly, demand for artificial intelligence business equipment and
transportation equipment drove an increase in durable goods orders in September on the
back of new orders for U.S.-manufactured capital goods, suggesting business spending

on equipment remained robust in Q3. Another factor, see Exhibit 5B, where critical
components such as generators, switchboards, chillers and transformers continue to
face long lead times that affect delivery timelines and ultimate profitability for new data
center development.
These signposts reinforce our view that we are only in the middle innings of the AI
investment cycle. From an investment perspective, huge demand for data centers, power
systems, networking infrastructure, specialized chips and other equipment continues

to strain supply, creating both bottlenecks and potential opportunities for companies
powering the buildout.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500/Market-cap Weighted Index is a market-capitalization-weighted index that is widely regarded as the best single gauge of large-cap U.S. equities. The index includes 500 leading

companies and covers approximately 80% of available market capitalization.
S&P 500 Equal-Weighted Index includes the same constituents as the capitalization weighted S&P 500, but each company in the S&P 500 EWI is allocated a fixed weight - or 0.2% of the
index total at each quarterly rebalance.
Bloomberg U.S. Aggregate Bond Index is a broad-based, market-capitalization-weighted benchmark that measures the performance of the U.S. investment-grade, U.S. dollar-denominated,

fixed-rate taxable bond market
Bloomberg Commodity Index is a broadly diversified financial benchmark that tracks the price movements of physical commodities through futures contracts.
MSCI Emerging Market Index captures large and mid cap representation across 24 Emerging Markets (EM) countries.
United Nations Food and Agriculture Food Price Index is a measure of the monthly change in international prices of a basket of food commodities.
Philadelphia Semiconductor Index is a market-cap-weighted index focusing on 30 leading semiconductor firms, offering insights into the tech market.

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

## Document 362: Eurozone_ Improving business sentiment, driven by tech, is spreading to other sectors (2026-10-14)

EUROZONE: IMPROVING BUSINESS SENTIMENT, DRIVEN BY TECH, IS SPREADING TO OTHER SECTORS
Stéphane Colliac & Tarik Rharrab
*LATEST OBSERVATION: 5 OCTOBER 2026  **ESTIMATE
The September PMI surveys confirm that growth in the Eurozone is getting stronger, boosted in particular by new export orders, which are
still rising. Sectors that were already performing well – technology and capital goods (including for defence) – are continuing to grow, while

those that had been struggling until now – aeronautics, consumer goods and financial services – are seeing their production indices recover.
This strong performance is particularly noteworthy, given that it has not yet been undermined by higher inflation.
The heatmap shows the PMI index readings: a reading above 50 indicates expansion, while a reading below 50 signals contraction compared to the previous
month. For each indicator, sectors are ranked in descending order based on their September 2026 reading.

Shades of red represent values above 50, while shades of blue represent values below 50: the intensity of the colour increases the further away they are
from this threshold.
Production growth spreads to more sectors
The PMI surveys on business sentiment confirm that business activity in the Eurozone is expanding at a faster pace and spreading to more sectors.
Household-consumption-related sectors (goods and services), which were adversely affected in the spring by the outbreak of the conflict in the Middle

East, do not appear to be negatively impacted by the rising inflation observed since August. The aeronautics sector, where production has mainly been
limited by supply constraints, is also showing signs of improvement.

These trends, although still moderate, come alongside a drive to upgrade equipment, fuelled by investment in digital technology, electrification
(which is supporting the automotive sector) and defence. The momentum in technology goods and services further strengthened in September.
The production index for computer and electronics hit a five-year high, while the software services production index stayed close to its July peak.

Financial services are also enjoying a significant improvement, reflecting the healthy lending momentum despite interest rates starting to rise
(see our analysis).
Growth driven by exports
Exports drove growth in the first half of 2026, both across the Eurozone as a whole (see our analysis) and among its key members (notably
Germany). This momentum is also increasing, as indicated by the ‘new export orders’ component of the various PMI indices. As was the case with

production, there has been a particularly noteworthy increase for capital goods (technology and machinery) and related services, particularly digital
services. This trend is playing out more broadly, as, in September, new export orders rose across all sub-sectors, with the exception of intermediate
goods and consumer goods, where contraction recently eased. There has been a significant improvement in the transport sector, particularly in
the aeronautics industry, which has major markets in Asia. Recent data on French exports show that aeronautics exports to this region are rising

once again.
The convergence between rising production and increasing export-order backlogs shows that Eurozone growth is based on an increasingly solid
foundation. Inflationary pressures are not holding it back for the time being.

## Document 371: FX Outlook 2027_ Seven key risks

Source PDF: all report pdf/FX Outlook 2027_ Seven key risks.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-12
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Low volatility masks a wide range of tail risks for 2027. AI stress,
another inﬂation shock, ﬁscal concerns, elections and
geopolitical escalation could all trigger sharp FX moves, while
ﬁnancial repression and a carry unwind add further asymmetric
risks

Chris Turner and Francesco Pesole
AI bust: expect unorthodox FX reactions
The resilience of the AI trade in equities has been tested of late, and
a more decisive unwind could become an overarching FX driver next
year. We think the impact of a major US tech selloﬀ on currencies
would depend on how far it spreads to broader US and global risk
assets, whether it heavily threatens ﬁnancial stability, and how it
impacts the inﬂation backdrop. Crucially, the initial FX reaction may
look very diﬀerent from the medium-term move.
The dollar still retains decent safe-haven appeal by our estimates,
having recovered from the 2025 “sell America” period. Yet episodes
in 2026 where AI concerns hit tech stocks without materially

spilling into broader equities were accompanied by some USD
underperformance. We think contained US tech-speciﬁc weakness
would continue to weigh moderately on the dollar next year, with
low-yielding haven currencies such as the yen, Swiss franc and euro
beneﬁting the most.
The dynamics would be diﬀerent if tech losses triggered a broader
selloﬀ. In that scenario, we would expect an initial safe-haven boost
to the dollar, followed by a larger depreciation as the shock

deepens and the Federal Reserve response comes into focus. The
greater the threat to ﬁnancial stability, the stronger the case for the
Fed to provide liquidity and prevent a global ﬁnancial crisis-style
credit crunch, ultimately weighing on the dollar. Over the medium
term, we would expect JPY, CHF and EUR to outperform, while the
Australian dollar, New Zealand dollar and non-European EM
currencies would be hit harder, consistent with recent AI-driven

selloﬀs (see chart below).
There is, however, an important inﬂation caveat. A severe risk-oﬀ
shock would itself reduce inﬂation risks through potential job
losses, negative wealth eﬀects and tighter credit conditions. But if
inﬂation remains elevated, the Fed may be slower to ease, not least
to preserve the safe-haven role of the Treasury market. In that
scenario, we think the dollar would outperform for longer, while
European currencies would be less insulated than under a more

benign inﬂation backdrop.

Asian currencies have been excluded due to time-zone mismatch
Source: ING, Reﬁnitiv
A new 2022 for inﬂation
Our baseline assumes contained second-round eﬀects from the
energy shock, allowing major European central banks to cut rates
towards the end of 2027. Four risks could challenge that view.
Second-round eﬀects may simply take longer to emerge, as a
prolonged crisis and higher gas prices erode corporate buﬀers and
feed into food and services inﬂation. Tighter labour markets could

amplify the shock through stronger wage growth, while renewed
Middle East escalation could drive another sharp rise in energy
prices. Finally, greater ﬁscal support could cushion the hit to
growth, but at the cost of higher medium-term inﬂation pressures.
In such a scenario, we would expect broad dollar gains. Historically,
low-yielders have tended to suﬀer most when Fed tightening drives
global yields higher, but we would be wary of applying that

playbook mechanically in 2027. The relative central bank response
should be the key diﬀerentiator, with those that have tightened less
so far retaining more ammunition to defend their currencies.
We still see the Norwegian krone and Swedish krona as particularly
vulnerable given their low liquidity and high beta, while AUD could
be a standout loser given the Reserve Bank of Australia's limited
room to tighten further amid domestic vulnerabilities. EUR and JPY,

by contrast, could prove more resilient than in 2022, provided the

### Page 4

European Central Bank and Bank of Japan respond swiftly to
renewed inﬂation pressures.
Current central bank rates vs latest peak
Source: ING, Macrobond
Fiscal takeover: contained, but
potentially explosive risk
We’ve all seen the French bond sell-oﬀ unfold over recent
months. The heart of the story is one of budget credibility. If
investors suspect that a government will not or cannot amend a
worrying budget trajectory, then they will demand higher

coupons or, in extremis, as in Greece 2010, refuse to buy
government debt. France is no Greece and our base case
assumes that some modest budget consolidation can keep
French spreads contained in 2027. Even if French politicians fail
to address investor concerns, the ECB’s Transmission Protection
Instrument (TPI) backstop can prevent broader contagion.
What about the US? One area of concern is that near 60% of US
Treasury issuance is in the form of short-term bill issuance, as

well as its large reﬁnancing requirements. What happens if
inﬂation spikes and the US Treasury has to pay ever-higher
reﬁnancing rates? Equally, what happens if White House policy
again upends the dollar-US Treasury safe-haven correlation as
was the case in March/April 2025? As we saw at the time, the
breakdown in these correlations can trigger some of the most

extreme dollar selling. And the fact that investors are again
running low FX hedge ratios on US assets warns of a similar

Debt ﬁnancing overview
Source: IMF, ING
Watch the EU election calendar!
2027 will be a busy year for eurozone political risk, with markets
focused primarily on ﬁscal implications and the threat of EU
political fragmentation. Expect any negative EUR impact to make
the Swiss franc a standout outperformer, thanks to its European

safe-haven role.
French elections combine both ﬁscal and EU-wide risks and are
likely to matter most. The biggest downside for the euro would
come from a surprise victory by Jean-Luc Mélenchon in the April-
May presidential election or a lack of a clear majority in potential
parliamentary elections over the summer. To a lesser extent, a
Marine Le Pen victory could also weigh on sentiment if early signals
point to a more confrontational approach towards the EU than

already expected.
In Germany, several state elections will be watched for signs of
greater government instability or further gains by the Eurosceptic
Alternative for Germany (AfD). Markets will focus particularly on the
implications for Germany’s reform agenda, and by extension the
growth outlook.

Italian elections rank third among our political risks. Although
scheduled by December, a May-June vote looks increasingly likely.
Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni remains favoured to secure another
mandate, potentially backed by a broader coalition. Italian asset
resilience has relied on Meloni’s ﬁscal discipline, so a surprise
victory by the centre-left bloc would, in our view, pose material
downside risks to BTPs and the euro.
A busy election calendar for next year

Source: ING, average of pollsters
Debasement trade strikes back
2025 was the year of dollar debasement, when fears over a
captured Fed led to pressure on the dollar against the Swiss franc,
while bitcoin and gold went bid. We had a ﬂash of that trade again
this August, when Scott Bessent’s extension of longer-dated US
Treasury buybacks unnerved the bond market and USD/CHF was hit
hard.
Were US Treasury yields to rise sharply next year, the theme of

‘ﬁnancial repression’ could re-emerge. Here the US Treasury could
try to contain Treasury yields by providing regulatory incentives to
banks to hold more Treasuries or put pressure on the Fed to
intervene. Any suggestion that the Treasury would not be allowing

Source: ING, Reﬁnitiv
The end of the carry trade?
Popular carry trade target currencies through 2026 have again
been the Turkish lira and the Nigerian naira. On the former, Turkish
presidential and parliamentary elections must be held by May
2028 and present some risk that the Central Bank of Turkey may

opt to change its policy of a controlled depreciation in the lira. That
would come as a big shock to the market. Nigeria looks slightly
better positioned after recent reforms to fuel subsidies and in the
energy sector.
When looking at the popular funding currencies of the yen, we
doubt slightly more aggressive BoJ tightening would be enough to
trigger a big short squeeze in the yen. And speculators are actually
running net long yen positions today as opposed to the large short

yen positions held in summer 2024 – the last time we had a major
adjustment in yen positioning.
The biggest driver of a carry trade unwind would probably come
through a ﬁnancial shock. Were any unwind in the AI trade to
uncover stresses or imbalances in, for example, the private credit
area and lead to ﬁnancial distress, volatility would surge and value-

### Page 8

at-risk considerations could prompt indiscriminate deleveraging.
We haven’t really seen much ﬁnancial distress since the Silicon
Valley Bank crisis of 2023.
A big carry trade unwind would be yen and Swiss franc positive,
while negative for the high-yielding activity currencies.
Geopolitical blowout: more than one
front to watch
There are three major fronts to watch in terms of geopolitical tail
risks next year. All of these would see the dollar as the big

outperformer.
A spillover of Russia’s war beyond Ukraine, or a material escalation
in cyber and hybrid attacks on European countries, would hit
European currencies the hardest. CEE currencies and the
Scandinavian bloc would be particularly exposed given their
geographical proximity and sensitivity to regional security risks.
The Swiss franc and yen would join the dollar in a big rally.
A broader Middle East conﬂict drawing in additional regional or

external powers would mainly hit FX through higher energy prices.
Net energy importers would suﬀer most, particularly currencies
where central banks have limited scope for additional tightening to
oﬀset the inﬂationary shock. Here, the dollar would be a clear
winner, as other low-yielders would suﬀer from the energy shock.
A sharp escalation in geopolitical tensions in East Asia would put
Asian currencies under heavy pressure, with AUD and NZD also

vulnerable as liquid proxies. European currencies would be relatively
more insulated from the initial shock. Regional risks could also
include US-China trade and technology tensions.
Are geopolitical risks the new normal?

## Document 378: GPIF、運用姿勢に変化なし ～2026年9月末の運用実績速報、国内債券の比率引き上げ観測は後退か～

Source PDF: all report pdf/GPIF、運用姿勢に変化なし ～2026年9月末の運用実績速報、国内債券の比率引き上げ観測は後退か～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-11-09
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Flash Insights

2026年 11月 9日

1/3 
GPIF、運用姿勢に変化なし 
～2026年 9月末の運用実績速報、国内債券の比率引き上げ観測は後退か～ 
 資産運用調査部 主席研究員  奥田 宏二

1. 2026年 7～9月の収益率はマイナス 1.65％ 
年金積立金管理運用独立行政法人（GPIF）は 2026年 11月6日、同年 7～9月の運用実績を公表
した。期間収益率はマイナス1.65％だった。7～9月は国内外の長期金利が上昇し、債券価格が下
落した。加えて円高も進み、外国資産の円換算額を押し下げた。資産別の収益率は国内債券がマ
イナス 1.80％、外国債券がマイナス6.52％、国内株式がプラス3.71％、外国株式がマイナス
1.86％となった。9月末の運用資産額は314兆3,876億円と6月末から約3.4兆円減少した。 
GPIFを巡っては、片山さつき財務・金融相が7月に「日本の金融資産にさらなる投資をしてい
ただくという方向で後押しをする方策を追求したい」と発言し、ポートフォリオに変化があるか
注目が集まっていた。長期金利が上昇していたこともあり、特に国内債への関心が高かった。 
結論から言うと、国内債比率は基本ポートフォリオの25％に極めて近く、運用姿勢の変化を読
み取ることは難しい。国内債の比率を引き上げるのではないかという観測は一旦後退するだろ
う。

2. 国内債券比率は 25％近辺を維持  
GPIFの基本ポートフォリオは国内外の株式と債券をそれぞれ25％とし、国内債券には±6ポイ
ントの乖離許容幅が設定されている。GPIFの公表資料によると、年金積立金全体に占める国内債
比率は 2026年9月末時点で26.49％だった。これには年金特別会計で管理する積立金、約 5.4兆
円が含まれている。 
この積立金を国内債券と年金積立金全体から除き、GPIFの運用資産ベースで計算すると、9月末
の国内債券比率は25.23％となる。6月末の24.98％から 0.25ポイント上昇したが、基本ポートフ
ォリオの25％をわずかに上回る水準だ（資料1）。過去の実績と比較しても、今回の比率は突出し
ていない。2020～2025年度の 9月末平均は 25.05％で、2026年9月末はこれを0.18ポイント上回
る程度だ。

### Page 2

2/3 
資料 1 GPIFの運用資産構成比率（推計、2026年 6月末・9月末）

（注）年金特別会計で管理する積立金を除いたGPIF運用資産ベース。 
（出所）GPIF「2026年度第 1四半期運用状況（速報）」、「同第 2四半期運用状況（速報）」より第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成。

3. 国内債券の買越額は推計 1.4兆円  
次に、7～9月の国内債券への買越額を推計する。6月末と9月末の国内債券残高の変化から、
期間中の運用損益による増減を除いて推計した。 
年金特別会計で管理する積立金を除いた国内債券残高は、6月末の79兆3,840億円から9月末
の79兆3,206億円へ、634億円減少した。一方、7～9月の国内債券の運用損益はマイナス1兆
4,452億円だった。この差額から、国内債券への買越額は約1.4兆円と推計される（注 1）。4～6
月の約 5.7兆円と比較すると、国内債券への資金配分額は減少した（注2）。 
同じ方法で他の資産への資金配分を試算すると、外国債券は約3.3兆円の買い越しとなった。
一方、国内株式は約2.0兆円、外国株式は約0.8兆円の売り越しとなった。

4. 国内債券の運用姿勢に変化はみられず  
2026年7～9月の運用実績をみる限り、GPIFが国内債券の乖離許容幅を積極的に活用し、基本ポ
ートフォリオの25％を上回る水準へと比率を引き上げようとする動きは確認できなかった。 
国内債券は買い越しているとみられるものの、外国債券には国内債券を上回る資金が配分され
ている。4資産の比率はいずれも25％近い。基本ポートフォリオに近づけて運用する従来の姿勢
が維持されていると考えられる。 
以 上

### Page 3

経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。
3/3
【注釈】
1) 買越額は国内債券残高の変化から運用損益を控除した推計値。国内債券には為替ヘッジ付き外
国債券や円建て短期資産が含まれるため、日本国債の純購入額とは必ずしも一致しない。
2) 2026年7～9月は、GPIFの運用資産額の増減から運用収益額を差し引くと、約1.9兆円の資金
流入があった計算になる。4～6月はほぼゼロだった。こうした資金流入の違いも、国内債券の
買越額を比較する際には考慮する必要がある。

tajinder.dhillon@lseg.com; 
THIS WEEK IN EARNINGS 
AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS 
 26Q3 Y/Y earnings are expected to be 31.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the Y/Y earnings estimate is 26.4%. 
 Of the 299 companies in the S&P 500 that have reported earnings to date for 26Q3, 81.6% have reported earnings 
above analyst estimates. This compares to a long-term average of 67.7% and prior four quarter average of 81.5%. 
 26Q3 Y/Y revenue is expected to be 13.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the growth estimate is 12.2%. 
 69.9% of companies have reported 26Q3 revenue above analyst expectations. This compares to a long-term 
average of 63% and an average over the past four quarters of 76.8%. 
 For 26Q3, there have been 40 negative EPS preannouncements issued by S&P 500 corporations compared to 66 
positive EPS preannouncements. By dividing 40 by 66 the N/P ratio is 0.6 for the S&P 500 Index.  
 The forward four-quarter (26Q4– 27Q3) P/E ratio for the S&P 500 is 19.0. 
 During the week of Nov. 2, 144 S&P 500 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings.

decreases in earnings, while the energy (15.7B) sector has recorded the highest dollar-level increase in earnings. 
Overall, expected share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 17.7B since the start of the quarter.

(since 1994), 67% of companies beat estimates and 20% miss estimates. Over the past four quarters, 82% of

The estimated earnings growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q3 is 31.6%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth 
rate declines to 26.4%. The S&P 500 expects to see share-weighted earnings of $778.4B in 26Q3, compared to share-
weighted earnings of $591.4B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 503 constituents) in 25Q3. 
11 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q3. The Energy and 
Information Technology sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Consumer 
Discretionary sector has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q3. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (146.2%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $63B in 26Q3, 
compared to earnings of $25.6B in 25Q3. 4 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (566.5%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (143.8%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 39%. 
The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (68.9%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $260.2B in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $154B in 25Q3. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (137%) and Semiconductor Materials & 
Equipment (71.5%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, 
the growth rate declines to 28.2%. 
The Consumer Discretionary sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (3.9%) of any sector. It is expected to earn 
$56.3B in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $54.2B in 25Q3. 7 of the 18 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to 
see lower earnings than a year ago. The Casinos & Gaming (-44.1%) and Automotive Parts & Equipment (-38.8%) 
sub-industries have the lowest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate 
improves to 4.5%.

the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 52.1%. 
The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (56.3%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $292.1B in 26Q4, compared to earnings of $186.8B in 25Q4. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (119.5%) and Semiconductor Materials & 
Equipment (72.3%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, 
the growth rate declines to 19.1%. 
The Industrials sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (3.9%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $52B in 26Q4, 
compared to earnings of $50.1B in 25Q4. 2 of the 20 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower earnings 
than a year ago. The Aerospace & Defense (-39.6%) and Passenger Airlines (-29.8%) sub-industries have the lowest 
earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 30.5%.

improves to 6%.

STARMINE EARNINGS SURPRISE FORECAST
Looking forward at quarterly performance, we use StarMine’s SmartEstimate® from LSEG to determine which
companies in the S&P 500 are better poised to beat and miss earnings estimates. The SmartEstimate® is a weighted

average of analyst estimates, with more weight given to more recent estimates and more accurate analysts. Our
studies have shown that when the SmartEstimate® differs significantly from the consensus (I/B/E/S mean estimate),
the Predicted Surprise accurately predicts the direction of earnings surprises or further revisions 70% of the time.
When a significant Predicted Surprise for revenue is also present for the period, the accuracy improves to 78%.

StarMine ARM from LSEG is an analyst revisions stock ranking model, designed to predict future changes in analyst
sentiment. ARM incorporates more accurate earnings estimates through the SmartEstimate prediction service. ARM
region rankings scores companies by region on a scale of 1 to 100 where 100 represents the most bullish sentiment.
Over the next two weeks, 147 S&P 500 companies are expected to report earnings. Of these companies, 17 positive

surprises and 11 negative surprises are expected from S&P 500 companies reporting quarterly results.

### Page 3

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
3
OVERVIEW
Coming into the home strait for 2026,
the Australian consumer has again
got the staggers. Spending has held
up surprisingly well given the intense
headwinds that have buffeted household
budgets over the year. But the same cannot
be said for sentiment which has fallen off
badly in the face of more shocks.

Our October Red Book surveys the scene.
Psychologically, consumers has seen a
return to the ‘cost of living nightmare’
they struggled with through much of
2022, 2023 and 2024. Global forces
and domestic factors are again having
pervasive negative impacts on budgets.
The scale may not be as severe as the
previous episode – inflation is running in
the 3-4% range not the 7-8% and interest
rates have risen 1ppt not 4 – but the
feeling is similar. In part this because there

has was very little reprieve in between –
despite tax cuts and a partial unwind of the
interest rate tightening in 2025, household
incomes never got a chance to regain the
lost ground.
The inflation ‘relapse’, and the difficulty
the RBA and governments seem to be
having in managing the situation, is likely
feeding into fears about the outlook for the
wider economy as well.
Exactly what this means for spending is
not entirely clear. As discussed in our last

report , sentiment reads have been much
weaker than actual spending in recent
years. The apparent disconnect is likely
due to a mix of legacy effects from COVID,
and the specific challenges posed by high
inflation (including the lingering effects
of high prices even once inflation has
subsided).
On balance we expect the spending side
to slow materially, particularly as we
head into year end. Indeed, the seven
weeks between now and Christmas
could prove decisive for the consumer

outlook, beginning with another important
sentiment survey update and then the high
season for retail. How spending performs
outside of key sales windows will be most
telling.
